# Concept Stubs: Y

## Yadkin River

### Summary
The Yadkin River is a major waterway in north-central North Carolina supporting important recreational catfish fisheries. This survey found abundant bullhead populations (mean CPUE 2.11 fish/minute) above Idols Dam with favorable habitat including wide channels (>38 m), numerous riffle areas, and gravel bottoms, while flathead catfish were confirmed absent above the dam but present below it.

### Context
> This 1987 survey assessed ictalurid (catfish family) populations in the [[Yadkin River]] and [[South Yadkin River]] above and below [[Idols Dam]] and [[Cooleemee Dam]] to determine whether [[Flathead Catfish]] (*Pylodictis olivaris*) should be stocked upstream of these barriers. Using...
> -- *[[SurveyOfIctaluridPopulationsIn_15266]]*

## Yadkin River Trail Association

### Summary
The Yadkin River Trail Association is an organization that improved fishing access by adding 13 sites on the Yadkin River and 2 on the South Yadkin River. This increased access led to greater fishing pressure, which factored into management decisions to preserve the abundant bullhead fishery above Idols Dam rather than introducing flathead catfish that could eliminate bullheads.

### Context
> This 1987 survey assessed ictalurid (catfish family) populations in the [[Yadkin River]] and [[South Yadkin River]] above and below [[Idols Dam]] and [[Cooleemee Dam]] to determine whether [[Flathead Catfish]] (*Pylodictis olivaris*) should be stocked upstream of these barriers. Using...

## Yaeger Canyon

### Summary
Yaeger Canyon is a tributary of East Clear Creek in Arizona that was surveyed during the 1990-1991 Little Colorado Spinedace assessment. Despite having perennial or semi-permanent water, no spinedace were found in this tributary during the survey period.

## Yakima River Basin

### Summary
The Yakima River Basin has the highest farmgate agricultural values in the United States after California's Central Valley. In 1999, American Rivers successfully convinced irrigation interests to recommend universal surface and groundwater metering to the Secretary of Interior and State of Washington, along with strong enforcement programs including stream masters and strategic penalties to protect salmon streams.

## Yalobusha River

### Summary
The Yalobusha River is a drainage system in central Mississippi where Procambarus lylei was discovered. Both known collection localities for this new crayfish species (Shutispear Creek and Toposhaw Creek in Calhoun County) are within this river drainage.

### Context

## Yamabe

### Summary
Yamabe is the name for river-resident cherry salmon that do not migrate to sea. In rearing experiments, 40 to 60% of fry from anadromous cherry salmon runs became Yamabe and failed to smolt, with males making up as much as 75% of them. Their egg output is much lower than that of anadromous females, at 200 to 400 eggs each.

### Context
> This Ontario Department of Lands and Forests Research Information Paper (Fisheries) No. 37 (February 1970, by W. J. ## Yampa Canyon
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Yampa Canyon refers to a river canyon section within the Yampa River system that serves as a critical study area for monitoring fish populations, habitat conditions, and channel dynamics in the Colorado River Basin. This geomorphologically significant reach has been the focus of long-term ecological research examining fish distribution, habitat use, growth rates, and physical channel changes in response to dam operations and natural flow regimes. The canyon represents an important conservation area for native fish species and their aquatic habitats within the western United States river systems.

## Yampa River
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Yampa River is a critical tributary of the Upper Colorado River Basin and designated critical habitat for four endangered native fish species, particularly the Colorado pikeminnow (Colorado squawfish), making it a focal system for recovery efforts addressing flow regimes, habitat restoration, and non-native species management. As an unregulated or partially regulated river segment, the Yampa River serves as a key reference ecosystem for understanding natural flow-ecology relationships and evaluating the impacts of introduced species like northern pike on native cyprinid populations. The river has been the subject of intensive technical assessment and recovery planning by the Colorado River Recovery Program to establish flow recommendations and habitat requirements necessary for the persistence of Upper Colorado River Basin endemics.

## Yampa River Basin Partnership

### Summary
The Yampa River Basin Partnership is a local group that signed a memorandum of understanding with the Fish and Wildlife Service to give local stakeholders a role in Yampa water and endangered fish decisions. Its planning objectives include late-summer flow needs for Colorado squawfish and humpback chub.

## Yampa River Basin Recovery and Water Management Plan

### Summary
The Yampa River Basin Recovery and Water Management Plan is a planning effort to balance endangered fish recovery needs with existing and future water development in the Yampa River Basin. The FY 1998 work plan allocated $398,000 for development of this plan, recognizing the Yampa River as the most important river in the Upper Colorado River Basin for maintenance and recovery of endangered fishes.

## Yampa River Projects Alternatives Feasibility Study

### Summary
The Yampa River Projects Alternatives Feasibility Study is an examination of reservoir options on the Yampa River to support endangered fish recovery and water management in the Upper Colorado River Basin. As of April 1990, CH2MHill had been selected as the contractor for the study, which was 4-6 weeks behind schedule though summer fieldwork remained on track, and the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service agreed to provide in-kind technical review services to offset budget shortfalls.

## Yankee Fork

### Summary
Yankee Fork is a tributary in the Salmon River drainage where spring-run Chinook salmon spawn in its upper reaches. Redd counts increased from 233 in 1965 to 454 in 1966, reflecting the overall increase in spring chinook spawning escapement.

## Yaqui Beautiful Shiner

### Summary
The Yaqui beautiful shiner (Cyprinella formosa) is a small, silvery cyprinid fish with orange to yellow-orange fins, listed as threatened in 1984. Historically occurring in southeastern Arizona and the Mimbres River Basin in southern New Mexico, it was re-introduced to the USA in 1990 when fish collected from Arroyo Moctezuma, Chihuahua, Mexico were stocked into Twin Pond on San Bernardino National Wildlife Refuge, where they are now reproducing and thriving in pond habitats. Full delisting cannot be determined until viable self-sustaining populations are demonstrably secure within the historic range in Mexico and populations are established and secure in both Arizona and New Mexico.

## Yaqui catfish
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Yaqui Catfish is an endangered fish species endemic to the Rio Yaqui basin, a transboundary watershed spanning Sonora, Mexico and the Arizona-Mexico border region, making it a subject of international conservation concern and recovery planning efforts by the Desert Fishes Recovery Team. As a native catfish of this arid-region river system, it faces threats from habitat degradation, water extraction, and competition from non-native species, necessitating coordinated monitoring and management protocols within the framework of U.S. Endangered Species Act recovery initiatives. The species represents an important component of the Rio Yaqui's endemic fish fauna, alongside other at-risk taxa such as the Yaqui Chub and Beautiful Shiner, and requires dedicated conservation attention due to its restricted geographic range and declining population status.

## Yaqui chub
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Yaqui Chub is a federally endangered fish endemic to the Yaqui River Basin in southeastern Arizona, representing a key species of conservation concern within the desert fishes recovery program managed by U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service and state agencies. As a native cyprinid adapted to the isolated Yaqui River system, this species exemplifies the broader conservation challenges facing desert aquatic ecosystems in the southwestern United States, where habitat loss, water diversions, and introduced species have severely depleted populations of endemic fishes. Recovery efforts for the Yaqui Chub have involved habitat management at protected refuges such as San Bernardino National Wildlife Refuge and coordination among multiple agencies to prevent extinction through both in-situ preservation and, when necessary, controlled population management interventions.

## Yaqui Longfin Dace
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Yaqui Longfin Dace (Agosia chrysogaster, "Yaqui form") is a native cyprinid fish endemic to the Rio Yaqui drainage system in Arizona, historically occurring in streams such as West Turkey Creek. Though not federally listed as endangered, this distinct form held Candidate 2 conservation status as of 1994 and shares critical habitat with the federally endangered Yaqui Chub, making it an important co-occurring species in regional conservation and habitat management efforts. Its presence in the same aquatic ecosystems as other endemic, at-risk species underscores the conservation significance of protecting intact riparian habitats in the Yaqui drainage basin.

The Yaqui River Basin is a transboundary aquatic ecosystem spanning the United States-Mexico border that represents a distinct biogeographic subdivision for desert fish conservation, hosting multiple imperiled endemic and native fish species requiring coordinated recovery efforts. This basin has been a focal point for the Desert Fishes Recovery Team and federal agencies managing endangered species populations, reflecting the complex conservation challenges posed by international water systems in arid regions. The Yaqui River Basin's fish assemblages are characterized by high endemism and vulnerability to habitat degradation, making it a priority area for recovery planning alongside other southwestern river systems such as the Gila and upper Colorado river basins.

### Summary
A river system in northern Mexico and southeastern Arizona that is home to the Yaqui chub, Yaqui topminnow, Mexican stoneroller, and longfin dace. ### Summary
The Yaqui Shiner is a small native fish species endemic to the Rio Yaqui drainage in southeastern Arizona, historically occurring in warm, shallow stream habitats with moderate current and sandy substrates. This species represents an important component of the native fish fauna in the Yaqui basin and has been subject to conservation assessment and recovery planning efforts alongside other endemic southwestern fishes threatened by habitat alteration and non-native species invasion. The Yaqui Shiner's ecological role and status within its limited range make it significant for understanding and managing the biodiversity of this arid region's aquatic ecosystems.

### Summary
The Yaqui Sucker (*Catostomus bernardini*) is a catostomid fish native to the Yaqui River drainage of southeastern Arizona and northern Mexico, classified as a peripheral species with a highly restricted range and limited habitat availability. This species has experienced significant population declines and local extirpations, as documented by failed reintroduction attempts in isolated water sources like Leslie Canyon spring, reflecting broader conservation challenges faced by endemic fishes in arid southwestern drainages. Its disappearance from historically occupied areas underscores the vulnerability of specialized suckers to habitat fragmentation, water scarcity, and the difficulties of establishing viable populations in marginal or artificially managed aquatic systems.

## Yaqui topminnow
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Yaqui Topminnow is a small-bodied livebearing fish species endemic to the Rio Yaqui basin in Arizona that is federally listed as endangered due to severe habitat loss, water extraction, and competition from invasive species, resulting in its restriction to isolated spring and stream refugia. As a focus of multi-agency native fish conservation efforts coordinated through groups like the Southeast Arizona Native Fish Team, the species represents a critical case study in desert aquatic ecosystem management and the challenges of recovering populations of endemic fishes in arid regions. Conservation strategies for the Yaqui Topminnow typically involve habitat restoration, captive propagation, and translocation to protected refuge populations to prevent extinction.

### Context

## Yaqui Watershed

### Summary
The Yaqui Watershed is a river basin extending from southeastern Arizona into Sonora, Mexico, where Yaqui Topminnow and Yaqui Chub once dominated stream and cienega habitats. Historical Yaqui fish habitats throughout this watershed have deteriorated drastically in both Arizona and Mexico due to groundwater depletion and agricultural development, with major U.S. drainages like Whitewater Draw and Black Draw now running dry 80% of the time.

### Context
> This document is an instream flow request submitted by the [[U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service]] to the [[Arizona Department of Water Resources]] for [[Leslie Creek]] within [[Leslie Canyon National Wildlife Refuge]]. ## Yardley Sewage Works

### Summary
Yardley Sewage Works is a sewage treatment plant that was the main source of pollution load to the River Cole. The river was fishless below the works, although clean tributaries held roach and sticklebacks.

The group of fish hatched in the same year, whose strength determines later catches. The source finds the 1926 year class exceptionally strong and identifies probable strong classes in 1936, 1942 and 1944, and it stresses that failure of two successive year classes could collapse the fishery.

### Context

## Year-Class Survival

### Summary
Year-class survival refers to how many fish from a given hatching year survive to later life stages, which is a key measure of population health. In this report, the authors compared fall young-of-the-year from 1985 and 1986 and found fall catch rates differed (2.0 vs. 6.0) but the following July rates were nearly equal (0.23 and 0.29). They concluded that fall young-of-the-year monitoring may not reliably indicate year-class strength, and they recommended a late-March seining survey to assess overwinter survival of squawfish young.

### Context
> This Annual Summary Report, prepared by [[BIO/WEST Inc]] for the [[Bureau of Reclamation]] (BOR) under Contract No. 6-CS-40-03980 (dated January 12, 1988), describes the 1987 fish survey of the Cataract Canyon region of the upper Colorado River basin. The study sought to locate spawning sites of...

## Year-Round Fishing

### Summary
Angling allowed throughout the year rather than only in a set season. The survey tested this in a six-mile experimental section of the Shenandoah and found no evidence it harmed the fishery, so the authors recommended keeping it open year-round.

## Yellow Bass
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Yellow Bass (*Morone mississippiensis*) is a non-native percichthyid fish species in the family Moronidae that has invaded western aquatic ecosystems, particularly in Arizona's Salt River Project canal system and associated constructed lentic habitats. In the context of conservation biology for the Colorado River Basin, Yellow Bass represents an invasive predator threat to native endangered species such as Bonytail and Colorado Squawfish, as it competes for resources and preys upon native fauna in both natural and ex-situ refuge settings. Its presence in fish survey collections indicates successful establishment in atypical habitats far from its native eastern North American range, where it contributes to the ecological degradation of native fish communities.

## Yellow Bullhead
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Yellow Bullhead (*Ameiurus natalis*) is a small catfish species (family Ictaluridae) native to eastern North America that has become established in western aquatic systems, particularly in the Colorado River Basin and associated reservoirs. In ecological contexts, it is documented as a non-native species in constructed and natural lentic habitats where it exhibits high tolerance to chemical stressors, including resistance to fish toxicants used in eradication efforts targeting invasive species. Its presence in western water bodies is of conservation concern because it may compete with or predate upon native fish species, particularly in managed refuge systems designed for endangered endemic fishes.

## Yellow Grub

### Summary
Yellow Grub (Clinostomum sp.) is a trematode parasite that appears as yellowish-white spots under the skin and deeply embedded in the flesh of freshwater fish. When the cyst wall breaks during fish dressing, the grub can be observed moving on the host's flesh, making it one of the more visible parasites encountered by anglers.

## Yellow Perch
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Yellow Perch (*Perca flavescens*) is a freshwater panfish species commonly classified in the panfish guild for fisheries management and conservation purposes, typically targeted at minimum population thresholds of 200 or more individuals per water body. As a food fish with moderate ecological and economic value, yellow perch inhabits littoral zones of lakes and reservoirs and is frequently included in fish community surveys, scale sampling studies, and post-reclamation restocking programs. The species serves as an important indicator of freshwater ecosystem health and represents a key component of balanced panfish communities in North American aquatic systems.

## Yellow Pike Perch

### Summary
Stizostedion vitreum, the yellow pike perch, a freshwater percid fish of New York. The source reports that females of 4.5 to 5 pounds lay 118,000 to 128,000 eggs, and that hatching takes seven days at 57° F.

## Yellowfin Tuna
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Yellowfin tuna (*Thunnus albacares*) is a pelagic fish species belonging to the genus *Thunnus* and represents one of the most commercially important tuna species globally. As a highly migratory, fast-growing predator of tropical and subtropical oceans, yellowfin tuna occupy a key position in marine food webs and serve as important indicators of ocean health and radionuclide accumulation in pelagic ecosystems. The species is of significant concern in fisheries management and conservation biology due to its high exploitation rates and vulnerability to overfishing across major fishing regions.

## Yellowstone River
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Yellowstone River represents a significant aquatic ecosystem in North America that has served as an important study site for understanding fish populations, community dynamics, and habitat-fish relationships in riverine environments. Multiple ichthyological studies conducted on this river have examined aspects such as fish food habits, species distribution patterns, and the impacts of various environmental factors on fish communities, making it a key system for fisheries science and conservation biology research. The river's fish assemblages, including species like suckers and other native fishes, have provided valuable data for understanding how environmental variables and habitat characteristics influence fish behavior, catchability, and ecological interactions.

### Context

## Yuma Clapper Rail
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Yuma Clapper Rail is a federally endangered bird species that inhabits wetland and riparian habitats associated with the Lower Colorado River system, which represents an important focal point for endangered species conservation efforts in the region. While not a fish species itself, the Yuma Clapper Rail is documented in ichthyology and aquatic ecology literature as a key indicator species for wetland habitat quality and health in river systems affected by dam operations, water management, and nonnative species introductions. Its conservation status and habitat requirements make it relevant to comprehensive aquatic ecosystem management and recovery planning efforts that address both fish and wildlife species in altered riverine systems.

## Yuma Clapper Rail Recovery Plan

### Summary
The Yuma Clapper Rail Recovery Plan was published in February 1983 for the threatened subspecies Rallus longirostris yumanensis, which inhabits the Lower Colorado River Basin. ## Yuma Cove
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Yuma Cove is a managed backwater habitat within Lake Mohave in the Lower Colorado River system that has been specifically engineered and manipulated for larval and juvenile fish production, particularly for native fish species conservation efforts. The cove's management has involved physical modifications such as berm fortification and liner placement, vegetation management through macrophyte introduction, and periodic reclamation using rotenone treatment to control predatory and competing species, especially odonate larvae. Light trap sampling and monitoring protocols have been employed to track reproductive success and evaluate the cove's effectiveness as a controlled propagation environment for endangered or threatened fish species in the regulated Colorado River ecosystem.

## Yuma Cove Backwater

### Summary
Yuma Cove Backwater is a 2.6-acre natural lakeside cove on Lake Mohave used as off-channel habitat for razorback sucker conservation. This source reports that 200 razorback suckers stocked there in 2013 grew to over 400 adults producing hundreds to thousands of young-of-year within a few years, though apparent overabundance led to removal of 80 adults and 2,877 young-of-year in 2019, and remote PIT-tag scanning achieved 90-100% detection of fish later harvested.

### Context

## Yuma Valley

### Summary
Yuma Valley refers to canal systems in Arizona that were compared to the Coachella Canal. After a 1974 pesticide spill, Yuma Valley canals showed similar species composition but different community structure, with much higher densities of small forage fish (10.84 fish/m²) and lower game fish biomass (32.8%) compared to Coachella Canal's 84.3% game fish biomass.

## Yuriria elliana

### Summary
A fossil species of the genus Yuriria, a Mexican cyprinid group, named in this dissertation from Pleistocene lake deposits in the Lerma basin. The source associates it with the western mountain biogeographic track.

### Context
> This 1980 University of Michigan doctoral dissertation by Michael Leonard Smith examines the evolutionary and ecological history of the freshwater fish fauna of the [[Rio Lerma Basin]] in the western [[Mesa Central]] of Mexico.
